### Slide 5
- What is
- Nanoencapsulation
- ?
- Nanoencapsulation
- is an advanced technology that involves enclosing bioactive compounds within
- nanoscale
- carriers.
- It enhances drug stability, bioavailability, and targeted delivery while minimizing side effects.
- In
- bioimaging
- ,
- nanoencapsulation
- improves contrast, enables precise diagnostics, and reduces toxicity.
- This technology is revolutionizing medicine by making treatments more effective and diagnostics more accurate.
- Fig. 1 Encapsulation

### Slide 6
- Features
- Fig. 2:
- Nanoencapsulation
- Very tiny particles (called nanoparticles) are used to cover or protect substances like medicines, nutrients, or vaccines
- Size: 1 to 100 nm
- The substance being encapsulated is often called the "core", while the outer protective layer is known as the "shell" or “carrier".

### Slide 7
- Advantages of
- Nanoencapsulation
- Better Protection
- Targeted Delivery
- 7
- Easier Storage, Transport and Stability
- Fewer Side Effects and Lower Toxicity

### Slide 8
- Mechanism of
- Nanoencapsulation
- 🛠 How
- Nanoencapsulation
- Works?
- Nanoencapsulation
- can be achieved through different methods, depending on the type of carrier and the intended application.
- 🔬 Major Mechanisms:
- Physical Methods
- – Spray drying,
- electrospinning
- , and
- coacervation
- to create
- nano
- -sized capsules.
- Chemical Methods
- – Emulsion techniques, polymerization, and sol-gel processes for stable encapsulation.
- Biological Methods
- – Use of natural carriers like proteins or polysaccharides for biocompatibility.

### Slide 9
- Techniques
- NANOEMULSIFICATION
- NANOLIPOSOMES
- 9
- Nanoemulsification
- is the process of creating extremely small oil-in-water or water-in-oil droplets, typically ranging from 10 to 200 nanometers in size.
- Nanoliposomes
- are
- nano
- -sized vesicles composed of
- phospholipid
- bilayers
- , similar to the structure of cell membranes. These vesicles can encapsulate both hydrophilic (water-loving) and hydrophobic (water-repelling) molecules.

### Slide 10
- Techniques
- ELECTROSPRAY
- NANOSTRUCTURE FORMATION THROUGH THE USE OF CYCLODEXTRINS
- 10
- Electrospray
- is a technique that uses an electric field to break a liquid solution into ultrafine charged droplets, which then solidify into nanoparticles as the solvent evaporates.
- Cyclodextrins
- (CDs) are cyclic oligosaccharides that can form inclusion complexes with hydrophobic molecules. These molecules fit inside the hollow, doughnut-shaped structure of
- cyclodextrins
- , protecting them from environmental factors such as heat, light, and oxidation.

### Slide 11
- Nanoencapsulation
- in
- Theragnosis
- 11
- It is the integration of both therapeutic and diagnostic functions into a single
- nanoparticle
- system, specifically in cancer treatment
- Examples:
- Liposomes
- (e.g.,
- Doxil
- ) are used for targeted chemotherapy delivery.
- Gold nanoparticles are used for imaging and
- photothermal
- therapy.

### Slide 12
- 12
- 1. Targeted Drug Delivery
- Designed to specifically target cancer cell
- 2. Controlled Release
- Controlled and sustained release to ensure a consistent therapeutic effect
- Key Functions
- Fig. 3:
- Theragnostic
- Nanoparticle

### Slide 13
- 13
- 3. Combination Therapy
- Can carry multiple therapeutic agents
- 4. Diagnostic Imaging
- Imaging agents in NPs enables real-time tumor monitoring (via MRI, CT scans etc)
- 5.
- Biosensing
- Nanocarriers
- can detect cancer biomarkers
- Key Functions
- Fig. 4:  Mechanism of cancer treatment

### Slide 14
- Nanoencapsulation
- in
- Immunisation
- 14
- Nanoencapsulation
- in vaccination utilizes nanoparticles (NPs) to encapsulate and transport vaccine antigens, improving their stability, immune response effectiveness, and targeting to particular immune cells, resulting in more vigorous immune reactions.

### Slide 15
- 15
- Process
- 1. Encapsulation of Antigens
- E
- ncapsulated within nanoparticles made from polymers, lipids, or inorganic materials.
- 2. Targeted Delivery
- E
- ngineered to target specific cells
- 3. Controlled Release
- Slow release of antigens ensures prolonged immune system stimulation, reducing the need for booster doses.
- Advantages
- P
- rotect antigens from degradation, thus, longer shelf lives and easier storage and transport
- T
- arget specific immune cells, and enhance antigen presentation, leading to robust and durable immune responses
- D
- eliver multiple vaccine components in a single formulation, simplifying vaccine production and administration
- Nanoencapsulation
- in
- Immunisation

### Slide 16
- Real-World Applications of
- Nanoencapsulation
- in
- Theragnosis

### Slide 4
- Nanotechnology is an arena of functional discipline for study, creation, design and uses of the materials and systems with unique capabilities enabled by the control of dimensions of matter at
- nanometre
- scale (1-100 nm).
- Nanotechnology
- Origin of nanometre
- The prefix ‘
- nano
- ’ is derived from the Greek word ‘
- nanos
- ’ which means ‘dwarf’ (‘very short man’ ).
- ‘Nano’ is used in scientific units to denote one-billionth (10-9) of the base
- unit.and
- a ‘
- nanometre
- ’ (nm) equals to one billionth of a
- metre
- (10-9 m).

### Slide 5
- Nanomaterials
- Nanomaterials
- (NMs)
- are typically between 1 and 100
- nanometres
- (nm) in size – with 1 nm being equivalent to one billionth of a
- metre
- (10-9 m).
- Figure 1:
- Comparison of
- nano
- size with other lengths
- [1]

### Slide 6
- Classification of
- nanomaterials
- Zero-dimensional (0-D) NMs
- : Materials with all the three dimensions at
- nanoscale
- are termed as 0-D NMs.
- Example: Nanoparticles, Quantum-dot.
- One-dimensional (1-D) NMs
- : Two of the three dimensions of the material at
- nanoscale
- are termed as 1-D NMs.
- Example:
- Nanorods
- , nanotubes,
- nano
- wires of        	metals and metal-oxides.
- Two-dimensional (2-D) NMs
- : Materials with one of the three dimensions at
- nanoscale
- are termed as 2-D NMs.
- Example:
- Nanofilms
- ,
- nano
- sheets, coatings etc.
- Three-dimensional (3-D) materials
- : Materials with all the three dimensions outside the
- nanoscale
- are termed as 3-D materials.
- Example: Composites
- Figure 2:
- Classification of
- nanomaterials
- [2]

### Slide 7
- Properties of
- Nanomaterials

### Slide 8
- Why materials’ properties change at the
- Nanoscale
- ?
- At the
- nanoscale
- , materials exhibit unique properties due to:
- 1) High Surface-to-Volume Ratio :
- A larger proportion of atoms reside on the surface, significantly affecting chemical reactivity, strength, and other physical properties.
- 2) Quantum Confinement:
- Electron motion becomes restricted at the atomic scale, altering optical, electrical, and magnetic behaviors.

### Slide 9
- High Surface Area to Volume Ratio
- In
- nanomaterials
- , a large number of atoms are on the surface compared to inside.
- This makes them more reactive and affects properties like strength, chemical activity, and heat behavior.
- Figure 3: Surface area [3]

### Slide 10
- Quantum Confinement
- When the length of a semiconductor is reduced to the same order as the
- exciton
- radius, i.e., to a few nanometers, quantum confinement effect occurs and the properties get modified.
- In Nano Crystals, the Electronic energy levels are not continuous as in the bulk but are discrete (finite density of states), because of the confinement of the electronic Wave function to the physical dimensions of the particles.
- Figure 4: Illustration of quantum confinement effect on the band gap of general semiconductors
- [4]

### Slide 11
- Fabrication of
- nanomaterials
- is carried out by two approaches:
- Top-down approach:
- Top-down approach involves the breaking down of the bulk material into
- nanosized
- structures or particles.
- Examples: Ball milling, Lithography, Laser ablation
- Bottom-up approach
- : It refers to the build up of a material from the bottom: atom-by-atom, molecule-by-molecule, or cluster-by cluster.
- It begins with assembling of atoms or molecules one by one to large 	structure.
- Examples:
- Sol-gel,
- Hydrothermal method,
- Chemical precipitation
- Solvothemal
- synthesis
- Green synthesis using plant extracts
- (eco-friendly)
- Fabrication of
- Nanomaterials
- Figure 5: Synthesis technique [5]

### Slide 15
- 1. Adsorption
- Adsorption
- is a surface phenomenon where pollutants (
- adsorbates
- ) from water accumulate on the surface of a solid material (adsorbent) without penetrating into its bulk.
- Physical Adsorption
- (Physisorption):
- Involves weak van der Waals forces
- Reversible and occurs at low temperatures
- Chemical Adsorption
- (Chemisorption):
- Involves chemical bonding
- Stronger and usually irreversible
- https://www.majordifferences.com/2013/02/difference-between-physical-adsorption.html

### Slide 16
- Adsorption
- Removes heavy metals, dyes, organic pollutants, and toxic ions
- Nanomaterials
- like
- graphene
- oxide, CNTs,
- Fe₃O
- ₄, and
- nano
- -zeolites show excellent adsorption capacities
- Enhanced due to high surface area, porosity, and functional groups
- Example Adsorbents:
- Activated carbon
- Metal oxide nanoparticles
- Clay-based
- nanocomposites
- Biomass-derived
- nanomaterials

### Slide 17
- Photocatalyst
- :
- The material that functions as a catalyst when exposed to light.
- (TiO2,
- ZnO
- ,
- CuO
- , Fe2O3 are widely used as
- photocatalysts
- )
- Photocatalysis
- :
- A photo-induced reaction which is accelerated by the presence 			of a catalyst
- 2.
- Photocatalysis
- or
- Photocatalytic
- activity
- (Degradation of organic pollutants)
- Dr. Akira
- Fujishima
- Dr. Kenichi Honda
- Break down organic dyes, pesticides, and pharmaceutical residues.
- Reaction under UV/Visible light → production of hydroxyl radicals → decompose pollutants into CO₂ and H₂O.
- Photocatalytic
- splitting of water is discovered by:  Akira
- Fujishima
- & Kenichi Honda in 1972
- .

### Slide 4
- Nanosensors
- Nanosensors are miniature devices, typically at the nanoscale (1-100 nanometers), designed to detect and measure changes in physical, chemical, or biological properties.
- Designed to detect and respond to various stimuli such as pressure, temperature, chemical compounds, or biological markers with high sensitivity.
- Figure 1:  Nanosensors application in different fields [1]

### Slide 5
- Key Characteristics of
- Nanosensors
- :
- Miniaturization:
- Nanosensors are incredibly small, allowing for precise measurements at the nanoscale.
- High Sensitivity:
- They can detect minute changes in a variety of parameters, and they can detect extremely low concentrations of
- analytes
- , making them ideal for applications requiring high sensitivity.
- Rapid Response:
- Due to their small size and high surface area,
- nanosensors
- provide rapid detection and response times.
- Low Sample Volume:
- Nanosensors require only tiny sample volumes, which is particularly beneficial in medical diagnostics.
- Figure 2: Characteristics of
- nanosensors
- [2]

### Slide 6
- Types of Nanosensors
- 6
- Figure 3: Types of
- nanosensors
- [3]

### Slide 7
- Nano-sensors for enabling contamination detection
- Detecting Microbial Contamination:
- Nano-sensors can detect bacteria like E. coli and Salmonella in food products with high sensitivity, aiding in proactive food safety management.
- Monitoring Chemical Residues:
- They can detect pesticide residues and other chemical contaminants in food, ensuring product safety.
- Packaging and Storage:
- Nano-sensors can be integrated into food packaging to monitor for potential contaminants and assess the food's quality during storage.
- Figure 4: Nano-sensors for contamination
- detection [4]

### Slide 10
- Nano-sensors for environmental measurements
- Pollution Detection:
- They detect heavy metals, pesticides, and volatile organic compounds.
- Water Quality Monitoring:
- Nanosensors can detect contaminants like lead, arsenic, and bacteria in water sources, providing crucial data for water quality management.
- Air Quality Monitoring:
- Nanosensors monitor air pollutants such as carbon monoxide, nitrogen dioxide, and particulate matter, contributing to efforts to reduce air pollution and its associated health risks.
- Figure 6 : Nano bio-sensors for environmental
- applications [5]

- Nanosensor
- Applications in Remote Sensing
- Healthcare:
- Nanosensors can be used to detect biomarkers in the body, monitor disease progression, and deliver targeted therapies.
- Industrial Processes:
- Nanosensors
- can be used to monitor parameters in manufacturing plants, track the performance of machinery, and ensure safety.
- Agriculture:
- Nanosensors
- can be used to monitor crop health, detect pests, and optimize irrigation systems.

### Slide 14
- Nano-sensors for communication
- Wireless Communication:
- Nanosensors
- often utilize wireless communication methods, such as electromagnetic waves, to transmit data.
- Nano-networks:
- Nanosensors
- can be interconnected to form
- nano
- -networks, allowing for collaborative data collection and transmission.
- Communication Protocols:
- Various protocols, such as those used in the Internet of Things (
- IoT
- ) and Fog computing, can be adapted for
- nanosensor
- communication.
- Challenges in Nano-communication:
- Energy efficiency, limited bandwidth, and the complexity of managing a large number of
- nanosensors
- in a network are challenges that researchers are addressing.

### Slide 9
- 9
- Lithium-Ion Batteries (
- LiBs
- )
- Components
- :
- Anode (Negative Electrode)
- : Usually made of graphite.
- Cathode (Positive Electrode)
- : Typically made of a lithium metal oxide.
- Electrolyte
- : A lithium salt dissolved in a solvent (allows lithium ions to move).
- Separator
- : A porous material that keeps the anode and cathode from touching.
- Figure 6:
- Mode of operation of
- LiBs
- [4]

### Slide 10
- Nanotechnology  in Electrodes
- Nanotechnology in Anodes
- 1) Nano-Silicon (Si):
- High lithium storage capacity
- Addresses volume expansion issues
- 2) Carbon Nanotubes (CNTs) &
- Graphene
- :
- Improve conductivity
- Create flexible support matrices
- Nanotechnology in Cathodes
- 1)
- Nanoscale
- Layered Oxides (
- LiCoO
- ₂):
- Enhanced lithium diffusion
- Improved electrochemical stability
- 2) Nano-coatings:
- Protect against electrolyte degradation
- Improve thermal stability
- Figure 7:
- I
- ntercalation of lithium-ions into the anode and cathode
- matrices upon charge and discharge
- [6]

### Slide 11
- 11
- Charging Process
- :
- Electrical energy is supplied.
- Lithium ions
- move
- from the cathode to the anode
- through the electrolyte.
- Electrons
- travel through the external circuit to the anode (providing charging current).
- Lithium ions embed (intercalate) into the graphite layers of the anode.
- Discharging Process
- :
- Battery powers a device.
- Lithium ions
- move
- from the anode to the cathode
- through the electrolyte.
- Electrons
- flow through the external circuit from anode to cathode (powering the device).
- Lithium ions return to the cathode structure.
- Working of Lithium ion Battery
- Figure 7:
- I
- ntercalation of lithium-ions into the anode
- and cathode matrices upon charge and discharge
- [6]

### Slide 13
- Solar cells
- 13
- A solar cell is a device that converts sunlight directly into electricity using the photovoltaic effect
- When sunlight hits the solar cell, it knocks electrons loose in the semiconductor material (usually silicon).
- These electrons flow through a circuit, creating an electric current.
- Figure 8:
- Solar panel
- [7]

### Slide 14
- Nanotechnology in Solar cells
- Quantum Dots
- Nanoscale
- semiconductor particles that can tune the absorption spectrum of solar cells.
- Potential to generate multiple electron-hole pairs from a single photon (multiple
- exciton
- generation), improving efficiency.
- Nanowires & Nanotubes
- Serve as conductive paths for electrons.
- Improve charge collection and reduce recombination losses.
- Enable thin-film solar cells with less material and lower cost.
- 14

- nanomaterials

- help create ultra-thin layers that still absorb efficiently.
- Makes solar panels flexible, lightweight, and potentially cheaper.
- Improved Efficiency and Durability
- Nanocoatings
- can make cells more resistant to weather and UV degradation.
- Some
- nanomaterials
- help in self-cleaning surfaces (hydrophobic
- nanocoatings

### Slide 4
- Nanocomposites
- Nanocomposites
- are materials that consist of a matrix (bulk material) reinforced with
- nanoscale
- components (fillers) to enhance their mechanical, electrical, thermal, or other properties. These
- nanoscale
- reinforcements typically have at least one dimension below 100 nanometers.
- Figure 1:
- Nanocomposite
- [1]

### Slide 5
- Key components of
- nanocomposites
- 1) Matrix
- Base material: polymer, metal, or ceramic.
- Provides structural framework.
- 2) Nano-filler
- Nanoparticles such as carbon nanotubes,
- graphene
- ,
- nanoclays
- , metal oxides, etc.
- Enhances mechanical, thermal, electrical, or barrier properties.

### Slide 7
- Types of
- Nanocomposites
- Used in Coatings
- Polymer
- Nanocomposites
- :
- These offer enhanced flexibility, scratch resistance, and UV protection. For instance, epoxy resin with silica nanoparticles improves durability and resistance to environmental degradation.
- Metal
- Nanocomposites
- :
- They improve conductivity and magnetic properties, useful in specialized coatings. Silver nanoparticles in a polymer coating can add antimicrobial properties and enhance conductivity.
- Ceramic
- Nanocomposites
- :
- They provide superior thermal and chemical stability, making them ideal for high-temperature applications. An example is zirconia nanoparticles in a silica matrix, enhancing hardness and stability.

### Slide 8
- Metal Oxides
- : High hardness, UV resistance, and chemical inertness (e.g., SiO2, TiO2, Al2O3,
- ZnO
- ). TiO2 nanoparticles enhance UV resistance, extending coating lifespan by 40%.
- Graphene
- :
- High strength, flexibility, and barrier properties.
- Graphene
- -enhanced coatings provide a 90% improvement in corrosion resistance.
- Nanoclays
- :
- Improved barrier properties and mechanical strength.
- Montmorillonite
- clay increases tensile strength of polymer composite by 30%.
- Carbon Nanotubes
- :
- Excellent mechanical strength, conductivity, and reinforcement. CNTs can increase tensile strength of epoxy resins by up to 150%.
- Common
- Nanomaterials
- Used

- allotropes of carbon [2]

### Slide 4
- Introduction to
- Nanodevices
- Nanodevices
- function within the 1–100 nanometer range, offering groundbreaking capabilities due to altered quantum and surface phenomena. Their compact nature enables integration into advanced circuits, sensors, and biomedical instruments. By exploiting unique
- nanoscale
- effects, these devices deliver faster speeds, reduced power consumption, and new functionalities unachievable with traditional micro-scale counterparts.

### Slide 5
- Basics of Semiconductor Physics
- Semiconductors possess a crucial energy band gap, allowing control over electrical conductivity. Through doping, carriers (electrons or holes) are introduced to form n-type or p-type materials. At the
- nanoscale
- , confinement of charge carriers leads to discrete energy levels, significantly impacting electronic, optical, and thermal behaviors relevant to device performance.
- Figure 1: n and p-type semiconductor

### Slide 6
- Introduction to Semiconductor
- nanodevices
- Semiconductor
- nanodevices
- are tiny electronic devices that exploit the electrical properties of semiconductor materials—like silicon (Si), gallium arsenide (
- GaAs
- ), or newer 2D materials such as
- graphene
- and molybdenum disulfide (
- MoS
- ₂)—at the
- nanoscale
- (typically <100 nm). These devices are foundational in modern electronics, quantum computing, sensing, and optoelectronics.
- Size-dependent Properties
- Quantum confinement effects dominate at the
- nanoscale
- , altering electronic and optical behavior.
- High Performance & Speed
- Reduced size leads to faster switching speeds and lower power consumption.
- Integration & Density
- Enables dense integration of millions to billions of devices on a single chip (Moore's Law in action).
- Key Characteristics of Semiconductor
- Nanodevices

### Slide 7
- Applications of semiconductor
- nanodevices
- 1) Electronics
- Ultra-fast processors and memory chips.
- Low-power and high-efficiency transistors (e.g.,
- FinFETs
- , GAAFETs).
- 2) Quantum Devices
- Qubits built from quantum dots or Josephson junctions.
- Spintronics using semiconductor nanostructures.
- 3) Optoelectronics
- Nano-LEDs, solar cells with quantum efficiency improvements.
- High-resolution image sensors.
- 4) Sensors
- Nano-FET biosensors (e.g., for glucose, cancer markers).
- Gas sensors based on nanowires or nanotubes.

### Slide 8
- Introduction to
- Nanomanipulators
- Nanomanipulators
- are precision tools or systems designed to manipulate objects at the nanometer scale (1–100 nm). Think of them as microscopic “robotic arms” or positioning systems that operate at atomic or molecular scales. They're essential in nanotechnology, especially for applications in material science, biology, semiconductor fabrication, and atomic-scale engineering.
- Figure 2 :
- TESCAN
- Nanomanipulatorfor
- X, Y and Z axis
- manipulation

### Slide 9
- Types of
- Nanomanipulators
- 9
- 1) AFM-Based
- Nanomanipulators
- Use Atomic Force Microscopy to move particles or measure forces.
- Can "drag" atoms across surfaces.
- 2) STM-Based
- Nanomanipulators
- Use Scanning Tunneling Microscopes.
- Famous for positioning individual atoms (e.g., IBM’s quantum corrals).
- 3) SEM/TEM-Based
- Nanomanipulators
- Operate inside Scanning or Transmission Electron Microscopes.
- Useful for real-time imaging during manipulation.
- 4) MEMS/NEMS
- Nanorobots
- Micro/
- nano
- -electromechanical systems integrated with manipulation capabilities.
- Can act as
- nanoscale
- grippers or pushers.

### Slide 10
- Applications of
- Nanomanipulators
- 10
- Field
- Use
- Nanofabrication
- Assembling
- nanoscale
- circuits or devices.
- Biology
- Manipulating DNA, proteins, viruses, or cells.
- Materials Science
- Studying mechanical properties (e.g., stress tests on nanotubes).
- Semiconductor Industry
- Failure analysis, circuit editing, or precision deposition.
- Quantum Research
- Positioning
- atoms
- /
- molecules
- for quantum
- device
- construction.

### Slide 11
- Case study of
- Nanomanipulators
- 11

- https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0957415824000618

### Slide 12
- Introduction to Nano-transistors
- 12
- A transistor is a switch that controls the flow of current in electronic circuits. A
- nano
- -transistor does the same, but:
- smaller than 100 nanometers.
- Exhibits quantum effects such as tunneling and quantized conductance.
- Often uses advanced materials like carbon nanotubes,
- graphene
- , or 2D semiconductors.
- Figure 4:
- Nanotransistor

### Slide 13
- Types of Nano-transistors
- Type
- Description
- Status
- FinFET (Fin Field-Effect Transistor)
- Uses a 3D fin-like structure for better control of current
- Industry standard (Intel, TSMC)
- GAAFET (Gate-All-Around FET)
- Channels are fully surrounded by the gate for better control
- Emerging (used in 3nm and beyond)
- CNTFET (Carbon Nanotube FET)
- Channel made of carbon nanotubes
- Research & early-stage applications
- TFET (Tunnel FET)
- Uses quantum tunneling for switching
- Low-power, experimental
- Single Electron Transistor (SET)
- Controls flow of one electron at a time
- Research, quantum computing
- Molecular Transistor
- Uses a single molecule as a channel
- Experimental, proof-of-concept

### Slide 14
- Working Principle of Nano-transistors
- 14
- A
- nano
- -transistor works like a switch:
- Gate
- controls the electric field.
- Source
- provides electrons.
- Drain
- collects them.
- The
- channel
- , just a few nanometers wide, allows current to pass when the gate voltage is applied.
- In
- nano
- -transistors, quantum effects—like
- tunneling
- and
- ballistic transport
- —become significant and must be accounted for in design.
- Figure 5: A double-gate
- FinFET
- device

### Slide 15
- Applications of Nano-transistors
- 15
- Field
- Use
- CPUs & GPUs
- Billions of nano-transistors on a single chip
- Mobile Devices
- Power-efficient chips in smartphones
- AI & ML Hardware
- High-density transistors for accelerators
- Quantum Computing
- Nano-scale control of electron states
- Flexible Electronics
- Nano-transistors on plastic or fabric substrates

- Case study of Nano-transistors

- Future
- Prospects
- of
- Semiconductor
- nanodevices
- Emerging
- nanodevices
- promise to revolutionize computing, health, and energy sectors. Integration with
- neuromorphic
- chips, AI processors, and quantum systems will boost performance. Research into biodegradable, flexible electronics and advanced 2D materials like
- MoS
- ₂ and black phosphorus could yield transformative breakthroughs across diagnostics, clean tech, and communication.

### Slide 4
- Solid-State Batteries
- Solid-state batteries are a type of rechargeable battery that uses a solid electrolyte instead of a liquid or gel one, offering potential advantages like increased safety, higher energy density, and faster charging
- This technology is seen as a promising alternative to traditional lithium-ion batteries, particularly for electric vehicles
- 4
- Figure 1: Solid state battery [1]

### Slide 5
- Components of Solid-State Batteries
- Cathode:
- The positive electrode, often made of lithium metal oxides (like LiCoO2, LiNiMnCoO2, or LiFePO4), is responsible for providing lithium ions during discharge.
- Anode:
- The negative electrode, traditionally made of lithium metal (pure lithium), is responsible for storing and releasing lithium ions during the charging and discharging process.
- Solid Electrolyte/Separator:
- This component replaces the liquid electrolyte and separator in traditional batteries. It facilitates the movement of lithium ions between the anode and cathode while also providing mechanical separation between them
- 5
- Figure 2: Solid state battery [2]

### Slide 7
- Superionic
- electrolyte
- Superionic
- electrolytes are solid materials with remarkably high ionic conductivity, comparable to or exceeding that of liquid electrolytes
- These electrolytes are characterized by their ability to rapidly transport ions, making them ideal for applications requiring high energy density and safety
- Examples of
- Superionic
- Electrolytes:
- Lithium
- Superionic
- Conductors
- Sodium
- Superionic
- Conductors
- Proton Conductors
- RbAg4I5
- 7

- Superionic
- electrolyte

- Non-flammable and non-volatile
- Corrosion resistance
- 2)
- Improved Thermal Stability
- Good thermal stability
- Reduced risk of thermal runaway
- 3)
- Reduced Leakage
- Solid state
- Longer cycle life
- 4) Reduced Leakage
- Wider electrochemical stability window
- Mechanical support

### Slide 2

- Objective 2.

- Objective 3.

### Slide 3
- CONTENTS
- Electronic devices
- Energy loss in electronic devices
- Energy pyramid
- Design
- New materials in reducing power loss
- Fabrication techniques that support low-energy device architecture
- Case study
- Future scope
- References

### Slide 4
- Electronic Devices
- Improving the performance of electronic devices while minimizing energy loss involves a combination of technological advancements and strategic design choices.
- Key areas include using high-efficiency components, optimizing circuit design, employing advanced power management techniques, and exploring new materials and architectures.
- https://unsplash.com/s/photos/light-bulb

### Slide 5
- Energy loss in electronic devices
- Resistive Losses:
- The flow of current through any component with resistance (like wires, resistors) results in power loss as heat
- Switching Losses:
- In electronic circuits that switch between on and off states (like transistors), the energy lost during the switching process is known as switching loss
- Leakage Currents:
- Even when a device is supposed to be off, there can be small leakage currents that dissipate energy
- Core Losses (in transformers and inductors):
- In devices like transformers and inductors, energy can be lost in the magnetic core due to phenomena like eddy currents and hysteresis
- Thermal Losses:
- Energy lost as heat due to various factors like resistance and switching can lead to increased operating temperatures, which can affect performance and lifespan

### Slide 6
- The Energy Pyramid
- 6

### Slide 7
- Design:
- Improved Circuit Design
- Sub-threshold Circuit Design: Designing circuits that operate in the sub-threshold region, where the voltage is below the threshold for traditional logic gates, can lead to extremely low power consumption.
- Adiabatic Logic: This involves using energy recovery techniques during the switching of circuit states, which can significantly reduce energy loss in digital logic circuits.
- Power Gating: Disconnecting parts of the circuit that are not being used to reduce standby power consumption is a common technique for improving energy efficiency.

### Slide 8
- Improving the performance of electronic devices:Strategies
- Improving the performance of electronic devices while minimizing energy loss is a critical goal in modern electronics, as it not only boosts efficiency but also extends the longevity of devices and reduces environmental impact. Here are several strategies that can be employed to achieve are explained below:
- Low Power Design
- Power-Efficient Materials
- Energy Harvesting
- Efficient Power Management

### Slide 9
- Low Power Design
- :
- Use of Low Power Components: Selecting components such as low-voltage transistors, low-power microcontrollers, and low-power logic gates can reduce energy consumption.
- Clock Gating: This technique involves turning off the clock signal to portions of the device when they are not in use. This reduces unnecessary power consumption in idle states.
- Dynamic Voltage and Frequency Scaling (DVFS): Adjusting the voltage and frequency according to the workload can reduce power consumption when the device is not under heavy load.

### Slide 10
- Power-Efficient Materials
- Graphene and Carbon Nanotubes: These materials exhibit excellent electrical conductivity and minimal heat loss, improving performance while reducing energy waste.
- Wide-Bandgap Semiconductors: Materials such as Gallium Nitride (GaN) and Silicon Carbide (SiC) allow devices to operate at higher temperatures and voltages, reducing energy loss in high-power applications.
- Superconducting Materials: Superconductors have zero electrical resistance at low temperatures, allowing for energy-efficient electronic components in specialized applications (e.g., MRI machines, quantum computing).

### Slide 11
- Energy Harvesting:
- Ambient Energy Sources: Energy harvesting techniques, such as capturing vibrations, thermal energy, or even solar power, can be used to power electronic devices without relying solely on external power sources.
- Piezoelectric and Thermoelectric Devices: These devices convert mechanical stress or heat gradients into electrical energy, making them useful in reducing dependence on batteries for small devices or sensors.
- 11

### Slide 12
- Efficient Power Management
- Power Management ICs (PMICs): These integrated circuits are designed to manage power distribution and reduce energy loss through optimized voltage regulation, reducing inefficiencies in power conversion.
- Energy-Efficient Power Supplies: Switching power supplies are more efficient than traditional linear power supplies, as they convert power with minimal loss, especially in high-power applications.

### Slide 13
- New materials in reducing power loss
- High-Temperature Superconductors (HTS):
- HTS materials offer the potential for zero-resistance electricity transmission, significantly reducing I²R losses
- Wide
- Bandgap
- Materials:
- These materials, like silicon carbide (
- SiC
- ) and gallium nitride (
- GaN
- ), are used in power electronics to improve switching and conduction losses, leading to higher efficiency and reduced cooling needs.
- Soft Magnetic Materials:
- Soft magnetic materials, like soft magnetic composite materials (SMCs), are used in cores of transformers and electric motors to minimize core losses, enhancing efficiency and reducing the size of equipment.
- High-Temperature Low-Sag Conductors (HTLS):
- HTLS materials are used in overhead lines, allowing for higher current-carrying capacity at higher temperatures, reducing the need for more costly upgrades and maintenance
- 13

### Slide 14
- Fabrication techniques that support low-energy device architecture
- Inkjet printing, screen printing, and laser scribing
- Roll-to-roll printing
- 3D printing
- Electrodeposition
- Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD) and Plasma-Enhanced CVD (PECVD)
- Metal-Assisted Chemical Etching (MACE)

- Biocompatibility – safe for use in medical and biological systems.

## 📘 LECTURE NOTES & SYLLABUS CONTENT


### Applications of Optical fiber Lecture notes
# Applications of Optical fiber Lecture notes

Optical fibers have a wide range of applications due to their ability to transmit large amounts of data quickly, securely, and over long distances with minimal loss. Below is a detailed breakdown of the main applications of optical fiber, categorized by industry:

1. Telecommunications & Internet

Applications:

- Broadband internet: High-speed connections (FTTH - Fiber to the Home).

- Long-distance telephony: Reduced signal loss makes long-haul communications more efficient.

- Cellular backhaul: Fiber connects cell towers to data centers.

- Submarine cables: Intercontinental communication relies on undersea fiber optic cables.

Benefits:

- High bandwidth and data rates.

- Low signal degradation over long distances.

- Resistance to electromagnetic interference (EMI).

2. Medical Industry

Optical fibers are used in diagnostic and surgical equipment due to their small size and flexibility.

Applications:

- Endoscopy: Fiber optics deliver light and image data inside the body for diagnostics.

- Laser surgeries: Fibers guide laser beams in minimally invasive procedures.

- Biomedical sensors: Measure parameters like pressure, temperature, or chemical levels inside the body.

Benefits:

- Minimally invasive diagnostics.

- Compact, flexible delivery systems.

- Real-time imaging and data collection.

3. Military & Aerospace

Used for secure, lightweight, and high-speed data transmission.

Applications:

- Secure communications: Immune to tapping and EMI.

- Guided missile systems: Fiber gyroscopes for navigation.

- Aircraft data systems: Lightweight alternative to copper wiring.

Benefits:

- High reliability in extreme environments.

- Reduced weight and bulk.

- Enhanced security and resistance to jamming.

4. Industrial & Sensing Applications

Fiber optics monitor environmental and structural parameters in harsh or hazardous environments.

Applications:

- Temperature and pressure sensing in oil and gas pipelines.

- Structural health monitoring in bridges, tunnels, and buildings.

- Industrial automation: Real-time control signals immune to EMI.

Benefits:

- Real-time and remote monitoring.

- Durable and chemical-resistant.

- High precision and sensitivity.

5. Broadcasting & Entertainment

Fiber optics are used for high-definition transmission of video and audio signals.

Applications:

- Live event broadcasting: Fast, high-quality video streaming.

- Cable TV distribution: Improved signal quality and channel capacity.

- Theater lighting and effects: Fiber optics create dynamic lighting designs.

Benefits:

- Supports 4K and 8K video transmission.

- Noise-free audio/video signal.

- Long-distance, low-latency delivery.

6. Data Centers & Networking

Data centers rely heavily on fiber optics for internal and external connectivity.

Applications:

- Server interconnects: High-speed links between servers and storage.

- Cloud computing: Fast and scalable backbone connectivity.

- Enterprise networks: Fiber-to-the-desk for high-speed access.

Benefits:

- Supports massive data throughput.

- Scalable infrastructure for growing demand.

- Low power consumption and high efficiency.

7. Transportation Systems

Fiber optics improve safety, automation, and efficiency in transport systems.

Applications:

- Railway signaling and control systems.

- Traffic monitoring and smart traffic lights.

- In-vehicle networks (especially in aircraft and high-end vehicles).

Benefits:

- Real-time communication and monitoring.

- Improved system reliability and automation.

- Reduces maintenance with passive components.

References

1. https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/application-of-optical-fiber/

2. https://www.cables-unlimited.com/10-uses-of-fiber-optic-cables/

3. https://www.holightoptic.com/optical-fiber/

4. https://www.hfcl.com/blog/optical-fiber-working-principle

### Applications of laser lecture notes
# Applications of laser lecture notes

Lasers (Light Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation) have a vast range of applications across numerous fields due to their unique properties such as coherence, monochromaticity, directionality, and high intensity. Here's a detailed overview of the major applications of lasers, categorized by field:

1. Medical Applications

Lasers are extensively used in medicine for both diagnostic and therapeutic purposes.

a. Surgery

- Laser scalpel: Used for precise cutting with minimal damage to surrounding tissues.

- Common types: CO₂ lasers (soft tissue), Nd:YAG lasers (deep tissue penetration).

- Applications: Eye surgery (LASIK), tumor removal, cosmetic surgery, dental procedures.

b. Ophthalmology

- LASIK surgery: Reshapes the cornea to correct vision (myopia, hyperopia, astigmatism).

- Photocoagulation: Treats retinal disorders like diabetic retinopathy.

- Capsulotomy: Corrects posterior capsule opacification after cataract surgery.

c. Dermatology

- Tattoo removal

- Hair removal

- Skin resurfacing and wrinkle treatment

- Vascular lesion treatment

2. Industrial Applications

Lasers are widely used in manufacturing and material processing.

a. Cutting and Welding

- High-power lasers like CO₂ and fiber lasers cut metals, plastics, and fabrics with precision.

- Laser welding: Joins metals with minimal heat distortion.

b. Engraving and Marking

- Used on metals, glass, ceramics, and plastics.

- Common in electronics, automotive parts, and packaging.

c. 3D Printing / Additive Manufacturing

- Selective Laser Sintering (SLS) and Direct Metal Laser Sintering (DMLS) fuse powdered materials layer by layer.

3. Communication

Lasers are integral in modern communication systems due to their high bandwidth and low signal loss.

a. Fiber Optic Communication

- Laser diodes transmit data through optical fibers.

- Enables high-speed internet and global telecommunications.

b. Free-Space Optical (FSO) Communication

- Used where fiber optics are impractical (e.g., between buildings or satellites).

4. Military and Defense

Lasers serve various roles in defense systems.

a. Range Finding and Target Designation

- Laser rangefinders measure distance accurately.

- Target designators guide precision-guided munitions.

b. Directed Energy Weapons

- High-energy lasers (HEL) under development to destroy drones, missiles, or enemy electronics.

c. LIDAR in Surveillance

- Light Detection and Ranging used for terrain mapping and object detection.

5. Scientific Research

Lasers play a critical role in experimental physics, chemistry, and biology.

a. Spectroscopy

- Identifies materials and measures atomic/molecular properties.

- Types: Raman spectroscopy, laser-induced breakdown spectroscopy (LIBS).

b. Interferometry

- Measures small displacements, refractive index changes, and surface irregularities.

- Used in gravitational wave detection (e.g., LIGO).

c. Cooling and Trapping of Atoms

- Laser cooling used in atomic clocks and quantum computing research.

6. Entertainment and Holography

Lasers are used for visual effects and information storage.

a. Laser Shows

- Common in concerts and events for vibrant light displays.

b. Holography

- Uses laser light to create 3D images.

- Applications in data storage, security (e.g., credit card holograms), and art.

7. Consumer Electronics

Lasers are embedded in everyday devices.

a. Optical Storage Devices

- CD, DVD, Blu-ray players use lasers to read and write data.

b. Barcode Scanners

- Retail stores use laser scanners for inventory and sales tracking.

8. Space and Remote Sensing

a. LIDAR Mapping

- Used in topographic mapping, forestry, and autonomous vehicle navigation.

b. Laser Altimetry

- Measures surface elevation (e.g., by Mars orbiters).

c. Deep-Space Communication

- NASA is testing laser-based communication for high-data-rate transmissions from spacecraft.

References

1. https://www.physics-and-radio-electronics.com/physics/laser/applicationsoflasers.html

2. https://www.iitk.ac.in/stc/Publications/DGoswami/marked_Chapter_07.pdf

3. https://www.bfs.de/EN/topics/opt/application-medicine-wellness/laser-applications/laser-applications_node.html

4. https://www.rp-photonics.com/laser_applications.html

### Slide 8
- Applications
- of
- Optical fiber
- 8
- Industry
- Application
- Description
- Telecommunications
- Internet & Data Transmission
- High-speed broadband and long-distance communication.
- Cable Television (CATV)
- Delivers high-quality digital TV signals.
- Telephone Networks
- Used in trunk lines and high-volume call centers.
- Medical
- Endoscopy
- Fiber optic cables allow doctors to see inside the human body.
- Laser Surgery
- Light is transmitted through fiber optics for precise operations.
- Biomedical Sensing
- Used in monitoring and diagnostics (e.g., oxygen levels).

### Slide 9
- Applications
- of
- Optical fiber
- 9
- Industry
- Application
- Description
- Industrial
- Inspection & Automation
- Fiber scopes check pipes, engines, and hard-to-reach areas.
- Industrial Sensing
- Monitors temperature, pressure, and strain in structures.
- Defense & Aerospace
- Data Links & Communications
- Secure and fast data transmission in aircraft and submarines.
- Missile Guidance Systems
- Transmit signals in harsh environments.
- Night Vision Systems
- Transmit images in surveillance and targeting systems.
- Broadcasting
- Live Event Coverage
- Transmits real-time high-definition video over long distances.

### Slide 10
- Applications
- of
- Optical fiber
- 10
- Industry
- Application
- Description
- Networking
- Local Area Networks (LANs)
- High-speed and secure data networks.
- Data Centers
- Backbone connections between servers and storage.
- Automotive
- Lighting & Safety Sensors
- Fiber optics used in lighting and advanced driver-assist systems (ADAS).
- Research & Education
- High-Speed Internet in Campuses & Labs
- Supports large-scale data transmission for scientific research.
- Optical Experiments
- Fiber optics used in laser and light transmission experiments.
- Security
- Perimeter Intrusion Detection
- Fiber optic sensors detect vibrations and movements along a fence or area.
- Surveillance Systems
- Transmit video data securely and without interference.
